# Claim ledger: cpp levels 4 and 5, blind audit round 2 (`cpp-4-5-r2`)

Commit read: `e41dbada`. 2548 rows: 2526 correct, 10 imprecise, 12 misleading.

One row per claim-bearing string (Stage 1 course, full depth): prose, questions, keys and slips, options and their `why`, hints, feedback lines, explains, summaries, model answers, rubric lines, the text of each code widget, and one row each for its solution and its hidden tests. Every string was read; the basis names the standard section, the book, the derivation or the run that settles it. "run:" means the concepts script `docs/reports/cpp-L4-5-concepts-r2.py`, whose C++ part compiles with the page's unit builder and worker code under YoWASP clang 21 (wasm32, `-O1`, libc++ hardening, no exceptions); PROGRAM, MUTANT, WIDGET, ASM and CC name its checks. Section numbers are C++17 (N4659). Rows written before any earlier review, audit or author report on these levels was opened.

| l14-vector | `summary.ideas[0]` | `std::vector<T>` is a growing array of one type: `push_back` appends, `size()` counts, `[]` reads unchecked and `at()` reads checked. | correct | as screens 1, 2, 7, 8; amortised doubling, copies < 2n (CLRS 17.4; Python for n to 5,000) |
| l14-vector | `summary.ideas[1]` | Elements sit in one block whose capacity doubles here when full. `reserve(n)` makes the room once, so a known count never moves the block. | correct | as screens 1, 2, 7, 8; amortised doubling, copies < 2n (CLRS 17.4; Python for n to 5,000) |
| l14-vector | `summary.ideas[2]` | Iterators mark places: `[begin(), end())` covers every element, and `erase` leaves the old iterator invalid, so use the one it returns. | correct | as screens 1, 2, 7, 8; amortised doubling, copies < 2n (CLRS 17.4; Python for n to 5,000) |
| l14-vector | `summary.formula.name` | Growth by doubling | correct | as screens 1, 2, 7, 8; amortised doubling, copies < 2n (CLRS 17.4; Python for n to 5,000) |
| l14-vector | `summary.formula.tex` | \text{capacity} = 1, 2, 4, 8, \ldots \qquad \text{copies for } n \text{ pushes} < 2n | correct | as screens 1, 2, 7, 8; amortised doubling, copies < 2n (CLRS 17.4; Python for n to 5,000) |
| l14-vector | `summary.formula.note` | So `push_back` costs a constant amount on average. | correct | as screens 1, 2, 7, 8; amortised doubling, copies < 2n (CLRS 17.4; Python for n to 5,000) |

| l23-own-smart-pointer | `summary.ideas[0]` | A smart pointer is a class whose destructor frees what it owns. A unique one deletes copying and moves ownership, leaving the source null. | correct | as screens 2, 4, 5, 6 |
| l23-own-smart-pointer | `summary.ideas[1]` | A shared pointer holds the object pointer and a pointer to a counter block that all owners share. The last owner deletes both. | correct | as screens 2, 4, 5, 6 |
| l23-own-smart-pointer | `summary.ideas[2]` | Add to the other count before dropping your own, so `a = a` is safe. Never build two shared pointers from one raw pointer. | correct | as screens 2, 4, 5, 6 |
| l23-own-smart-pointer | `summary.formula.name` | The shared count | correct | as screens 2, 4, 5, 6 |
| l23-own-smart-pointer | `summary.formula.tex` | \texttt{copy: ++*count\_} \quad\quad \texttt{destroy: if (--*count\_ == 0) delete both} | correct | as screens 2, 4, 5, 6 |
| l23-own-smart-pointer | `summary.formula.note` | One counter, shared by every owner. | correct | as screens 2, 4, 5, 6 |
| l23-own-smart-pointer | `summary.trap` | Two shared pointers built from one raw pointer have two counters, so the object is deleted twice. | correct | as screens 2, 4, 5, 6 |

| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].prose` | Asks queue by price, lowest first, then by time, earliest first. `Order` has `price` and `time`. | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].question` | Which comparator sorts them? | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].widget.options[0].text` | `[](const Order& a, const Order& b) { return a.price != b.price ? a.price < b.price : a.time < b.time; }` | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].widget.options[1].text` | `[](const Order& a, const Order& b) { return a.price <= b.price; }` | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].widget.options[1].why` | `<=` is not strict, and it ignores time. Equal prices answer true both ways, which breaks `sort`. | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].widget.options[2].text` | `[](const Order& a, const Order& b) { return a.price != b.price ? a.price > b.price : a.time < b.time; }` | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].widget.options[2].why` | `>` puts the highest price first. That is the order for bids, not asks. | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].widget.options[3].text` | `[](const Order& a, const Order& b) { return a.time < b.time; }` | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].widget.options[3].why` | That sorts by time alone, ignoring price. | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].feedback.correct` | Price first, time on a tie, both strict. | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].feedback.generic` | Not quite right. Which rule puts the lowest price first and breaks ties by time? | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
| review-4 | `items[r4-comparator].explain.text` | For asks the best price is the lowest, so price uses `<`; a tie falls back to the earlier time. Both comparisons are strict, as `sort` requires. | correct | strict weak ordering [alg.sorting]/3; price-time priority for asks |
